stage=initial_retrievalrank=10query_text=For v/c ≪ 1 and ζ ≲ 1, what asymptotic form is given for Ξ0(y, τ)?node_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4:text:10point_id=6ee47e44-81ad-5c3f-b00a-8c003dc5e393doc_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4source=data\demo_docs\2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effect.pdftitle=2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effectpage=2chunk_index=10modality=textchannel=vectorvector_name=textparser_name=minerusource_parser=mineruscore=0.5021score_vector=0.5423score_bm25=nullscore_rrf=0.0161rerank_score=nullscore_composite=0.5021score_policy=weighted_v1threshold_passed=Trueexpanded_from=nullexpansion_relation=nullcandidate_pool=nullexpansion_mode=nullexpansion_allowed_by=nullseed_rank=nullseed_threshold=nullrelation_priority=nullreplaced_relation=nullagent_relevance_score=nullagent_relevance_label=nullagent_relevance_keep=nullagent_relevance_drop=nullagent_label_score_delta=nullagent_related_modality_delta=nullagent_related_context_text_delta=nullagent_related_context_node_id=nullagent_related_context_present=nullagent_related_context_added=nullagent_related_context_fixed_score=nullagent_relevance_reasoning=null
rerank_score=null
\tag {13}
$$

Recall that $\tau = \omega(t - x/v)$ and the cross section in the two-dimensional case has the dimension of length. Let us show that in the stationary case the expression (13) goes over to the known result. To do this, we set $\omega \to 0$ before the change of variables, and obtain the differential scattering cross section for the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect [1, 11]

$$
d \sigma = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P} \sin^ {2} Q \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}}. \tag {14}
$$

For $v/c \ll 1$ and $\zeta \lesssim 1$ the main contribution to the cross section is determined by the integration region $y \ll 1$ and $z \ll 1$ . In this case we find the asymptotics $\Xi_{0}(y, \tau)$ of the function $\Xi(y, \tau)$ ,

$$
\Xi_ {0} (y, \tau) = Q \operatorname{sgn} (y) \cos \tau \exp (- | y | / \vartheta),
$$

$$
\vartheta = \frac {v}{c \zeta} = \frac {\theta}{\theta_ {0}}, \quad \theta_ {0} = \frac {\hbar \omega}{2 E}. \tag {15}
$$

Using integration by parts and the asymptotics (15), we write the expression for the cross section in the form

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| s G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta} G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
\sigma_ {0} = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P \theta_ {0}} = \frac {2 v}{\pi \omega}, \quad \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) = Q e ^ {- y} \cos \tau . \tag {16}
$$

The function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ changes sign when replacing $\cos \tau \to -\cos \tau$ , while $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ does not change sign. For convenience, in Eq. (16) we have passed from the angle $\theta$ to $\vartheta$ .

If $\vartheta \gg 1$ ( $1 \gg \theta \gg \theta_0$ ), then

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \sin^ {2} (Q \cos \tau) \frac {d \vartheta}{\vartheta^ {2}}. \tag {17}

\tag {17}
$$

For $\vartheta \ll 1$ ( $\theta \ll \theta_0$ ), we have

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| \int_ {0} ^ {Q | \cos \tau |} (1 - \cos y) \frac {d y}{y} \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta . \tag {18}
$$

The asymptotics (17) and (18) for the cross sections are independent of $s = \operatorname{sgn}(q)$ , since either the function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ contributes to the cross section for $\vartheta \gg 1$ , or $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ for $\vartheta \ll 1$ . However, for $\vartheta \sim 1$ an asymmetry arises due to the interference of the functions $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ and $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ . This asymmetry will be an alternating function of $\tau$ and can be observed by measuring the non-stationary Hall effect (oscillating voltage in the transverse direction).

Let us represent the cross section $d\sigma(\vartheta, \tau)$ in (16) in the form

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = d \sigma_ {s} (\vartheta , \tau) + s d \sigma_ {a} (\vartheta , \tau),
$$

$$
d \sigma_ {s} (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left[ G _ {1} ^ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta^ {2}} G _ {2} ^ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right] d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
d \sigma_ {a} (\theta , \tau) = 2 \sigma_ {0} G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \frac {d \vartheta}{\vartheta}. \tag {19}
$$

The functions $G_{1}(\vartheta,\tau)$ and $G_{2}(\vartheta,\tau)$ depend on the parameter Q and variable $\tau$ only through the combination $f = Q\cos\tau$ . Fig. 1 shows the dependence of $\Sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau) = \sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ and $\Sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau) = \sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ on $\vartheta$ for several values of f.

stage=initial_expandedrank=6query_text=For v/c ≪ 1 and ζ ≲ 1, what asymptotic form is given for Ξ0(y, τ)?node_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4:text:10point_id=6ee47e44-81ad-5c3f-b00a-8c003dc5e393doc_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4source=data\demo_docs\2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effect.pdftitle=2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effectpage=2chunk_index=10modality=textchannel=vectorvector_name=textparser_name=minerusource_parser=mineruscore=0.5021score_vector=0.5423score_bm25=nullscore_rrf=0.0161rerank_score=nullscore_composite=0.5021score_policy=weighted_v1threshold_passed=Trueexpanded_from=nullexpansion_relation=nullcandidate_pool=nullexpansion_mode=nullexpansion_allowed_by=nullseed_rank=nullseed_threshold=nullrelation_priority=nullreplaced_relation=nullagent_relevance_score=nullagent_relevance_label=nullagent_relevance_keep=nullagent_relevance_drop=nullagent_label_score_delta=nullagent_related_modality_delta=nullagent_related_context_text_delta=nullagent_related_context_node_id=nullagent_related_context_present=nullagent_related_context_added=nullagent_related_context_fixed_score=nullagent_relevance_reasoning=null
rerank_score=null
\tag {13}
$$

Recall that $\tau = \omega(t - x/v)$ and the cross section in the two-dimensional case has the dimension of length. Let us show that in the stationary case the expression (13) goes over to the known result. To do this, we set $\omega \to 0$ before the change of variables, and obtain the differential scattering cross section for the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect [1, 11]

$$
d \sigma = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P} \sin^ {2} Q \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}}. \tag {14}
$$

For $v/c \ll 1$ and $\zeta \lesssim 1$ the main contribution to the cross section is determined by the integration region $y \ll 1$ and $z \ll 1$ . In this case we find the asymptotics $\Xi_{0}(y, \tau)$ of the function $\Xi(y, \tau)$ ,

$$
\Xi_ {0} (y, \tau) = Q \operatorname{sgn} (y) \cos \tau \exp (- | y | / \vartheta),
$$

$$
\vartheta = \frac {v}{c \zeta} = \frac {\theta}{\theta_ {0}}, \quad \theta_ {0} = \frac {\hbar \omega}{2 E}. \tag {15}
$$

Using integration by parts and the asymptotics (15), we write the expression for the cross section in the form

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| s G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta} G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
\sigma_ {0} = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P \theta_ {0}} = \frac {2 v}{\pi \omega}, \quad \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) = Q e ^ {- y} \cos \tau . \tag {16}
$$

The function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ changes sign when replacing $\cos \tau \to -\cos \tau$ , while $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ does not change sign. For convenience, in Eq. (16) we have passed from the angle $\theta$ to $\vartheta$ .

If $\vartheta \gg 1$ ( $1 \gg \theta \gg \theta_0$ ), then

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \sin^ {2} (Q \cos \tau) \frac {d \vartheta}{\vartheta^ {2}}. \tag {17}

Let us show that in the stationary case the expression (13) goes over to the known result. To do this, we set $\\omega \\to 0$ before the change of variables, and obtain the differential scattering cross section for the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect [1, 11]\n\n$$\nd \\sigma = \\frac {2 \\hbar}{\\pi P} \\sin^ {2} Q \\frac {d \\theta}{\\theta^ {2}}. \\tag {14}\n$$\n\nFor $v/c \\ll 1$ and $\\zeta \\lesssim 1$ the main contribution to the cross section is determined by the integration region $y \\ll 1$ and $z \\ll 1$ . In this case we find the asymptotics $\\Xi_{0}(y, \\tau)$ of the function $\\Xi(y, \\tau)$ ,\n\n$$\n\\Xi_ {0} (y, \\tau) = Q \\operatorname{sgn} (y) \\cos \\tau \\exp (- | y | / \\vartheta),\n$$\n\n$$\n\\vartheta = \\frac {v}{c \\zeta} = \\frac {\\theta}{\\theta_ {0}}, \\quad \\theta_ {0} = \\frac {\\hbar \\omega}{2 E}. \\tag {15}\n$$\n\nUsing integration by parts and the asymptotics (15), we write the expression for the cross section in the form\n\n$$\nd \\sigma (\\theta , \\tau) = \\sigma_ {0} \\left| s G _ {1} (\\vartheta , \\tau) + \\frac {1}{\\vartheta} G _ {2} (\\vartheta , \\tau) \\right| ^ {2} d \\vartheta ,\n$$\n\n$$\nG _ {1} (\\vartheta , \\tau) = \\int_ {0} ^ {\\infty} d y \\sin [ \\Xi_ {1} (y, \\tau) ] \\sin (\\vartheta y),\n$$\n\n$$\nG _ {2} (\\vartheta , \\tau) = \\int_ {0} ^ {\\infty} d y \\Xi_ {1} (y, \\tau) \\sin [ \\Xi_ {1} (y, \\tau) ] \\sin (\\vartheta y),\n$$\n\n$$\n\\sigma_ {0} = \\frac {2 \\hbar}{\\pi P \\theta_ {0}} = \\frac {2 v}{\\pi \\omega}, \\quad \\Xi_ {1} (y, \\tau) = Q e ^ {- y} \\cos \\tau . \\tag {16}\n$$\n\nThe function $G_{1}(\\vartheta, \\tau)$ changes sign when replacing $\\cos \\tau \\to -\\cos \\tau$ , while $G_{2}(\\vartheta, \\tau)$ does not change sign. For convenience, in Eq. (16) we have passed from the angle $\\theta$ to $\\vartheta$ .\n\nIf $\\vartheta \\gg 1$ ( $1 \\gg \\theta \\gg \\theta_0$ ), then\n\n$$\nd \\sigma (\\vartheta , \\tau) = \\sigma_ {0} \\sin^ {2} (Q \\cos \\tau) \\frac {d \\vartheta}{\\vartheta^ {2}}. \\tag {17}",

\tag {17}
$$

For $\vartheta \ll 1$ ( $\theta \ll \theta_0$ ), we have

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| \int_ {0} ^ {Q | \cos \tau |} (1 - \cos y) \frac {d y}{y} \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta . \tag {18}
$$

The asymptotics (17) and (18) for the cross sections are independent of $s = \operatorname{sgn}(q)$ , since either the function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ contributes to the cross section for $\vartheta \gg 1$ , or $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ for $\vartheta \ll 1$ . However, for $\vartheta \sim 1$ an asymmetry arises due to the interference of the functions $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ and $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ . This asymmetry will be an alternating function of $\tau$ and can be observed by measuring the non-stationary Hall effect (oscillating voltage in the transverse direction).

Let us represent the cross section $d\sigma(\vartheta, \tau)$ in (16) in the form

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = d \sigma_ {s} (\vartheta , \tau) + s d \sigma_ {a} (\vartheta , \tau),
$$

$$
d \sigma_ {s} (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left[ G _ {1} ^ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta^ {2}} G _ {2} ^ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right] d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
d \sigma_ {a} (\theta , \tau) = 2 \sigma_ {0} G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \frac {d \vartheta}{\vartheta}. \tag {19}
$$

The functions $G_{1}(\vartheta,\tau)$ and $G_{2}(\vartheta,\tau)$ depend on the parameter Q and variable $\tau$ only through the combination $f = Q\cos\tau$ . Fig. 1 shows the dependence of $\Sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau) = \sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ and $\Sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau) = \sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ on $\vartheta$ for several values of f.

It follows from the asymptotics obtained that for alternating current the total scattering cross section $\sigma(\tau)$ is finite, in contrast to the case of direct current. The main contribution to $\sigma(\tau)$ is given by the region of scattering angles $\theta \sim \t

mically with increasing $Q$ for large $Q$ .

# III. CONCLUSION

The non-stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect is investigated in the non-relativistic approximation. Using the eikonal approach, the differential and total scattering cross sections are found. In contrast to the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect, the total cross section in the non-stationary case is finite. The main contribution to the scattering cross section comes from the angles $\theta \sim \theta_{0} = \hbar\omega/E \ll 1$ . The differential scattering cross section contains an asymmetry with respect to the replacement $q \rightarrow -q$ . This asymmetry has a maximum at $\theta \sim \theta_{0}$ and decreases rapidly at $\theta \gg \theta_{0}$ and $\theta \ll \theta_{0}$ . The asymmetry in the cross section can be observed as the non-stationary Hall effect, and the total scattering cross section manifests itself in oscillations of the total electron flux.

# ACKNOWLEDGEMENT

stage=rerankrank=2query_text=For v/c ≪ 1 and ζ ≲ 1, what asymptotic form is given for Ξ0(y, τ)?node_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4:text:10point_id=6ee47e44-81ad-5c3f-b00a-8c003dc5e393doc_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4source=data\demo_docs\2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effect.pdftitle=2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effectpage=2chunk_index=10modality=textchannel=vectorvector_name=textparser_name=minerusource_parser=mineruscore=0.5912score_vector=0.5423score_bm25=nullscore_rrf=0.0161rerank_score=0.9287score_composite=0.5912score_policy=weighted_v1threshold_passed=Trueexpanded_from=nullexpansion_relation=nullcandidate_pool=nullexpansion_mode=nullexpansion_allowed_by=nullseed_rank=nullseed_threshold=nullrelation_priority=nullreplaced_relation=nullagent_relevance_score=nullagent_relevance_label=nullagent_relevance_keep=nullagent_relevance_drop=nullagent_label_score_delta=nullagent_related_modality_delta=nullagent_related_context_text_delta=nullagent_related_context_node_id=nullagent_related_context_present=nullagent_related_context_added=nullagent_related_context_fixed_score=nullagent_relevance_reasoning=null
\tag {13}
$$

Recall that $\tau = \omega(t - x/v)$ and the cross section in the two-dimensional case has the dimension of length. Let us show that in the stationary case the expression (13) goes over to the known result. To do this, we set $\omega \to 0$ before the change of variables, and obtain the differential scattering cross section for the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect [1, 11]

$$
d \sigma = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P} \sin^ {2} Q \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}}. \tag {14}
$$

For $v/c \ll 1$ and $\zeta \lesssim 1$ the main contribution to the cross section is determined by the integration region $y \ll 1$ and $z \ll 1$ . In this case we find the asymptotics $\Xi_{0}(y, \tau)$ of the function $\Xi(y, \tau)$ ,

$$
\Xi_ {0} (y, \tau) = Q \operatorname{sgn} (y) \cos \tau \exp (- | y | / \vartheta),
$$

$$
\vartheta = \frac {v}{c \zeta} = \frac {\theta}{\theta_ {0}}, \quad \theta_ {0} = \frac {\hbar \omega}{2 E}. \tag {15}
$$

Using integration by parts and the asymptotics (15), we write the expression for the cross section in the form

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| s G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta} G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
\sigma_ {0} = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P \theta_ {0}} = \frac {2 v}{\pi \omega}, \quad \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) = Q e ^ {- y} \cos \tau . \tag {16}
$$

The function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ changes sign when replacing $\cos \tau \to -\cos \tau$ , while $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ does not change sign. For convenience, in Eq. (16) we have passed from the angle $\theta$ to $\vartheta$ .

If $\vartheta \gg 1$ ( $1 \gg \theta \gg \theta_0$ ), then

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \sin^ {2} (Q \cos \tau) \frac {d \vartheta}{\vartheta^ {2}}. \tag {17}

stage=rerankrank=4query_text=For v/c ≪ 1 and ζ ≲ 1, what asymptotic form is given for Ξ0(y, τ)?node_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4:text:11point_id=048858d8-cbb7-533b-84fe-710d67b7c972doc_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4source=data\demo_docs\2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effect.pdftitle=2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effectpage=3chunk_index=11modality=textchannel=vectorvector_name=textparser_name=minerusource_parser=mineruscore=0.4815score_vector=0.4658score_bm25=nullscore_rrf=0.0149rerank_score=0.6944score_composite=0.4815score_policy=weighted_v1threshold_passed=Trueexpanded_from=nullexpansion_relation=nullcandidate_pool=nullexpansion_mode=nullexpansion_allowed_by=nullseed_rank=nullseed_threshold=nullrelation_priority=nullreplaced_relation=nullagent_relevance_score=nullagent_relevance_label=nullagent_relevance_keep=nullagent_relevance_drop=nullagent_label_score_delta=nullagent_related_modality_delta=nullagent_related_context_text_delta=nullagent_related_context_node_id=nullagent_related_context_present=nullagent_related_context_added=nullagent_related_context_fixed_score=nullagent_relevance_reasoning=null
\tag {17}
$$

For $\vartheta \ll 1$ ( $\theta \ll \theta_0$ ), we have

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| \int_ {0} ^ {Q | \cos \tau |} (1 - \cos y) \frac {d y}{y} \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta . \tag {18}
$$

The asymptotics (17) and (18) for the cross sections are independent of $s = \operatorname{sgn}(q)$ , since either the function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ contributes to the cross section for $\vartheta \gg 1$ , or $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ for $\vartheta \ll 1$ . However, for $\vartheta \sim 1$ an asymmetry arises due to the interference of the functions $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ and $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ . This asymmetry will be an alternating function of $\tau$ and can be observed by measuring the non-stationary Hall effect (oscillating voltage in the transverse direction).

Let us represent the cross section $d\sigma(\vartheta, \tau)$ in (16) in the form

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = d \sigma_ {s} (\vartheta , \tau) + s d \sigma_ {a} (\vartheta , \tau),
$$

$$
d \sigma_ {s} (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left[ G _ {1} ^ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta^ {2}} G _ {2} ^ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right] d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
d \sigma_ {a} (\theta , \tau) = 2 \sigma_ {0} G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \frac {d \vartheta}{\vartheta}. \tag {19}
$$

The functions $G_{1}(\vartheta,\tau)$ and $G_{2}(\vartheta,\tau)$ depend on the parameter Q and variable $\tau$ only through the combination $f = Q\cos\tau$ . Fig. 1 shows the dependence of $\Sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau) = \sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ and $\Sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau) = \sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ on $\vartheta$ for several values of f.

It follows from the asymptotics obtained that for alternating current the total scattering cross section $\sigma(\tau)$ is finite, in contrast to the case of direct current. The main contribution to $\sigma(\tau)$ is given by the region of scattering angles $\theta \sim \t

However, for $\\vartheta \\sim 1$ an asymmetry arises due to the interference of the functions $G_{1}(\\vartheta, \\tau)$ and $G_{2}(\\vartheta, \\tau)$ . This asymmetry will be an alternating function of $\\tau$ and can be observed by measuring the non-stationary Hall effect (oscillating voltage in the transverse direction).\n\nLet us represent the cross section $d\\sigma(\\vartheta, \\tau)$ in (16) in the form\n\n$$\nd \\sigma (\\vartheta , \\tau) = d \\sigma_ {s} (\\vartheta , \\tau) + s d \\sigma_ {a} (\\vartheta , \\tau),\n$$\n\n$$\nd \\sigma_ {s} (\\theta , \\tau) = \\sigma_ {0} \\left[ G _ {1} ^ {2} (\\vartheta , \\tau) + \\frac {1}{\\vartheta^ {2}} G _ {2} ^ {2} (\\vartheta , \\tau) \\right] d \\vartheta ,\n$$\n\n$$\nd \\sigma_ {a} (\\theta , \\tau) = 2 \\sigma_ {0} G _ {1} (\\vartheta , \\tau) G _ {2} (\\vartheta , \\tau) \\frac {d \\vartheta}{\\vartheta}. \\tag {19}\n$$\n\nThe functions $G_{1}(\\vartheta,\\tau)$ and $G_{2}(\\vartheta,\\tau)$ depend on the parameter Q and variable $\\tau$ only through the combination $f = Q\\cos\\tau$ . stage=agent_chunk_gradingrank=2query_text=For v/c ≪ 1 and ζ ≲ 1, what asymptotic form is given for Ξ0(y, τ)?node_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4:text:10point_id=6ee47e44-81ad-5c3f-b00a-8c003dc5e393doc_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4source=data\demo_docs\2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effect.pdftitle=2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effectpage=2chunk_index=10modality=textchannel=vectorvector_name=textparser_name=minerusource_parser=mineruscore=0.6912score_vector=0.5423score_bm25=nullscore_rrf=0.0161rerank_score=0.9287score_composite=0.6912score_policy=agent_chunk_label_delta_v1threshold_passed=Trueexpanded_from=nullexpansion_relation=nullcandidate_pool=nullexpansion_mode=nullexpansion_allowed_by=nullseed_rank=nullseed_threshold=nullrelation_priority=nullreplaced_relation=nullagent_relevance_score=1.0000agent_relevance_label=strongagent_relevance_keep=Trueagent_relevance_drop=Falseagent_label_score_delta=0.1agent_related_modality_delta=nullagent_related_context_text_delta=nullagent_related_context_node_id=nullagent_related_context_present=nullagent_related_context_added=nullagent_related_context_fixed_score=nullagent_relevance_reasoning=Explicitly states the regime and the resulting asymptotics Xi_0(y, tau).
\tag {13}
$$

Recall that $\tau = \omega(t - x/v)$ and the cross section in the two-dimensional case has the dimension of length. Let us show that in the stationary case the expression (13) goes over to the known result. To do this, we set $\omega \to 0$ before the change of variables, and obtain the differential scattering cross section for the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect [1, 11]

$$
d \sigma = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P} \sin^ {2} Q \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}}. \tag {14}
$$

For $v/c \ll 1$ and $\zeta \lesssim 1$ the main contribution to the cross section is determined by the integration region $y \ll 1$ and $z \ll 1$ . In this case we find the asymptotics $\Xi_{0}(y, \tau)$ of the function $\Xi(y, \tau)$ ,

$$
\Xi_ {0} (y, \tau) = Q \operatorname{sgn} (y) \cos \tau \exp (- | y | / \vartheta),
$$

$$
\vartheta = \frac {v}{c \zeta} = \frac {\theta}{\theta_ {0}}, \quad \theta_ {0} = \frac {\hbar \omega}{2 E}. \tag {15}
$$

Using integration by parts and the asymptotics (15), we write the expression for the cross section in the form

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| s G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta} G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
\sigma_ {0} = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P \theta_ {0}} = \frac {2 v}{\pi \omega}, \quad \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) = Q e ^ {- y} \cos \tau . \tag {16}
$$

The function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ changes sign when replacing $\cos \tau \to -\cos \tau$ , while $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ does not change sign. For convenience, in Eq. (16) we have passed from the angle $\theta$ to $\vartheta$ .

If $\vartheta \gg 1$ ( $1 \gg \theta \gg \theta_0$ ), then

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \sin^ {2} (Q \cos \tau) \frac {d \vartheta}{\vartheta^ {2}}. \tag {17}

This equation is independent of spin orientation. Using the method of characteristics, we find the solution to Eq. (9),

$$
F (x, y, t) = \exp \left\{- i \frac {e y}{c \hbar} \int_ {- \infty} ^ {x} d z \frac {A (x , y , t + (z - x) / v)}{\sqrt {y ^ {2} + z ^ {2}}} \right\}, \tag {10}
$$

where v = P/M. The shift in the last argument of the function $A(x, y, t)$ corresponds to the retardation effect. Note that the wave function (8), with $F(x,y,t)$ given by (10), satisfies the condition (6).

To obtain the scattering cross section, it is necessary to find the limit

$$
S (y, t ^ {\prime}) = \lim _ {x \to \infty} F (x, y, t)
$$

at a fixed value $t' = t - x / v$ . Replacing in (10) the upper limit of the integral with $\infty$ , we get

$$
S (y, t ^ {\prime}) = e ^ {- i \Xi (y, t ^ {\prime})},
$$

$$
\Xi (y, t ^ {\prime}) = \frac {e y}{c \hbar} \int_ {- \infty} ^ {\infty} \frac {d z}{\sqrt {y ^ {2} + z ^ {2}}}   A (x, y, t ^ {\prime} + z / v)  , \tag {11}
$$

Since the field is time-dependent, the particle flux (cross-section $d\sigma$ ) is also time-dependent. Following the standard derivation, see [11], we obtain

$$
d \sigma (\theta , t) = \frac {P}{2 \pi \hbar} \left| \int_ {- \infty} ^ {+ \infty} d y e ^ {- i q y} \left[ 1 - e ^ {- i \Xi (y, t ^ {\prime})} \right] \right| ^ {2} d \theta , \tag {12}
$$

Making the substitutions of variables $|q|y \to y$ , $|q|z \to z$ and $\omega t' \to \tau$ , we get

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \frac {\hbar}{2 \pi P} \left| \int_ {- \infty} ^ {+ \infty} d y e ^ {- i s y} \left[ 1 - e ^ {- i \Xi (y, \tau)} \right] \right| ^ {2} \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}},
$$

$$
\Xi (y, \tau) = \frac {\zeta y Q}{2} \int_ {- \infty} ^ {\infty} \frac {d z}{r} \left[ J _ {1} (\zeta r) \sin (\phi) - N _ {1} (\zeta r) \cos (\phi) \right],
$$

$$
\zeta = \frac {k}{| q |} = \frac {\hbar k}{P \theta}, Q = \frac {e \Phi_ {0}}{2 c \hbar}, \phi = \tau + \frac {c}{v} \zeta z,
$$

$$
r = \sqrt {y ^ {2} + z ^ {2}}, \quad s = \operatorname{sgn} q. \tag {13

stage=evidence_gaterank=2query_text=For v/c ≪ 1 and ζ ≲ 1, what asymptotic form is given for Ξ0(y, τ)?node_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4:text:10point_id=6ee47e44-81ad-5c3f-b00a-8c003dc5e393doc_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4source=data\demo_docs\2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effect.pdftitle=2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effectpage=2chunk_index=10modality=textchannel=vectorvector_name=textparser_name=minerusource_parser=mineruscore=0.6912score_vector=0.5423score_bm25=nullscore_rrf=0.0161rerank_score=0.9287score_composite=0.6912score_policy=agent_chunk_label_delta_v1threshold_passed=Trueexpanded_from=nullexpansion_relation=nullcandidate_pool=nullexpansion_mode=nullexpansion_allowed_by=nullseed_rank=nullseed_threshold=nullrelation_priority=nullreplaced_relation=nullagent_relevance_score=1.0000agent_relevance_label=strongagent_relevance_keep=Trueagent_relevance_drop=Falseagent_label_score_delta=0.1agent_related_modality_delta=nullagent_related_context_text_delta=nullagent_related_context_node_id=nullagent_related_context_present=nullagent_related_context_added=nullagent_related_context_fixed_score=nullagent_relevance_reasoning=Explicitly states the regime and the resulting asymptotics Xi_0(y, tau).
\tag {13}
$$

Recall that $\tau = \omega(t - x/v)$ and the cross section in the two-dimensional case has the dimension of length. Let us show that in the stationary case the expression (13) goes over to the known result. To do this, we set $\omega \to 0$ before the change of variables, and obtain the differential scattering cross section for the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect [1, 11]

$$
d \sigma = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P} \sin^ {2} Q \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}}. \tag {14}
$$

For $v/c \ll 1$ and $\zeta \lesssim 1$ the main contribution to the cross section is determined by the integration region $y \ll 1$ and $z \ll 1$ . In this case we find the asymptotics $\Xi_{0}(y, \tau)$ of the function $\Xi(y, \tau)$ ,

$$
\Xi_ {0} (y, \tau) = Q \operatorname{sgn} (y) \cos \tau \exp (- | y | / \vartheta),
$$

$$
\vartheta = \frac {v}{c \zeta} = \frac {\theta}{\theta_ {0}}, \quad \theta_ {0} = \frac {\hbar \omega}{2 E}. \tag {15}
$$

Using integration by parts and the asymptotics (15), we write the expression for the cross section in the form

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| s G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta} G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
\sigma_ {0} = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P \theta_ {0}} = \frac {2 v}{\pi \omega}, \quad \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) = Q e ^ {- y} \cos \tau . \tag {16}
$$

The function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ changes sign when replacing $\cos \tau \to -\cos \tau$ , while $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ does not change sign. For convenience, in Eq. (16) we have passed from the angle $\theta$ to $\vartheta$ .

If $\vartheta \gg 1$ ( $1 \gg \theta \gg \theta_0$ ), then

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \sin^ {2} (Q \cos \tau) \frac {d \vartheta}{\vartheta^ {2}}. \tag {17}

This equation is independent of spin orientation. Using the method of characteristics, we find the solution to Eq. (9),

$$
F (x, y, t) = \exp \left\{- i \frac {e y}{c \hbar} \int_ {- \infty} ^ {x} d z \frac {A (x , y , t + (z - x) / v)}{\sqrt {y ^ {2} + z ^ {2}}} \right\}, \tag {10}
$$

where v = P/M. The shift in the last argument of the function $A(x, y, t)$ corresponds to the retardation effect. Note that the wave function (8), with $F(x,y,t)$ given by (10), satisfies the condition (6).

To obtain the scattering cross section, it is necessary to find the limit

$$
S (y, t ^ {\prime}) = \lim _ {x \to \infty} F (x, y, t)
$$

at a fixed value $t' = t - x / v$ . Replacing in (10) the upper limit of the integral with $\infty$ , we get

$$
S (y, t ^ {\prime}) = e ^ {- i \Xi (y, t ^ {\prime})},
$$

$$
\Xi (y, t ^ {\prime}) = \frac {e y}{c \hbar} \int_ {- \infty} ^ {\infty} \frac {d z}{\sqrt {y ^ {2} + z ^ {2}}}   A (x, y, t ^ {\prime} + z / v)  , \tag {11}
$$

Since the field is time-dependent, the particle flux (cross-section $d\sigma$ ) is also time-dependent. Following the standard derivation, see [11], we obtain

$$
d \sigma (\theta , t) = \frac {P}{2 \pi \hbar} \left| \int_ {- \infty} ^ {+ \infty} d y e ^ {- i q y} \left[ 1 - e ^ {- i \Xi (y, t ^ {\prime})} \right] \right| ^ {2} d \theta , \tag {12}
$$

Making the substitutions of variables $|q|y \to y$ , $|q|z \to z$ and $\omega t' \to \tau$ , we get

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \frac {\hbar}{2 \pi P} \left| \int_ {- \infty} ^ {+ \infty} d y e ^ {- i s y} \left[ 1 - e ^ {- i \Xi (y, \tau)} \right] \right| ^ {2} \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}},
$$

$$
\Xi (y, \tau) = \frac {\zeta y Q}{2} \int_ {- \infty} ^ {\infty} \frac {d z}{r} \left[ J _ {1} (\zeta r) \sin (\phi) - N _ {1} (\zeta r) \cos (\phi) \right],
$$

$$
\zeta = \frac {k}{| q |} = \frac {\hbar k}{P \theta}, Q = \frac {e \Phi_ {0}}{2 c \hbar}, \phi = \tau + \frac {c}{v} \zeta z,
$$

$$
r = \sqrt {y ^ {2} + z ^ {2}}, \quad s = \operatorname{sgn} q. \tag {13

\tag {13}
$$

Recall that $\tau = \omega(t - x/v)$ and the cross section in the two-dimensional case has the dimension of length. Let us show that in the stationary case the expression (13) goes over to the known result. To do this, we set $\omega \to 0$ before the change of variables, and obtain the differential scattering cross section for the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect [1, 11]

$$
d \sigma = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P} \sin^ {2} Q \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}}. \tag {14}
$$

For $v/c \ll 1$ and $\zeta \lesssim 1$ the main contribution to the cross section is determined by the integration region $y \ll 1$ and $z \ll 1$ . In this case we find the asymptotics $\Xi_{0}(y, \tau)$ of the function $\Xi(y, \tau)$ ,

$$
\Xi_ {0} (y, \tau) = Q \operatorname{sgn} (y) \cos \tau \exp (- | y | / \vartheta),
$$

$$
\vartheta = \frac {v}{c \zeta} = \frac {\theta}{\theta_ {0}}, \quad \theta_ {0} = \frac {\hbar \omega}{2 E}. \tag {15}
$$

Using integration by parts and the asymptotics (15), we write the expression for the cross section in the form

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| s G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta} G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
\sigma_ {0} = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P \theta_ {0}} = \frac {2 v}{\pi \omega}, \quad \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) = Q e ^ {- y} \cos \tau . \tag {16}
$$

The function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ changes sign when replacing $\cos \tau \to -\cos \tau$ , while $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ does not change sign. For convenience, in Eq. (16) we have passed from the angle $\theta$ to $\vartheta$ .

If $\vartheta \gg 1$ ( $1 \gg \theta \gg \theta_0$ ), then

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \sin^ {2} (Q \cos \tau) \frac {d \vartheta}{\vartheta^ {2}}. \tag {17}

This equation is independent of spin orientation. Using the method of characteristics, we find the solution to Eq. (9),

$$
F (x, y, t) = \exp \left\{- i \frac {e y}{c \hbar} \int_ {- \infty} ^ {x} d z \frac {A (x , y , t + (z - x) / v)}{\sqrt {y ^ {2} + z ^ {2}}} \right\}, \tag {10}
$$

where v = P/M. The shift in the last argument of the function $A(x, y, t)$ corresponds to the retardation effect. Note that the wave function (8), with $F(x,y,t)$ given by (10), satisfies the condition (6).

To obtain the scattering cross section, it is necessary to find the limit

$$
S (y, t ^ {\prime}) = \lim _ {x \to \infty} F (x, y, t)
$$

at a fixed value $t' = t - x / v$ . Replacing in (10) the upper limit of the integral with $\infty$ , we get

$$
S (y, t ^ {\prime}) = e ^ {- i \Xi (y, t ^ {\prime})},
$$

$$
\Xi (y, t ^ {\prime}) = \frac {e y}{c \hbar} \int_ {- \infty} ^ {\infty} \frac {d z}{\sqrt {y ^ {2} + z ^ {2}}}   A (x, y, t ^ {\prime} + z / v)  , \tag {11}
$$

Since the field is time-dependent, the particle flux (cross-section $d\sigma$ ) is also time-dependent. Following the standard derivation, see [11], we obtain

$$
d \sigma (\theta , t) = \frac {P}{2 \pi \hbar} \left| \int_ {- \infty} ^ {+ \infty} d y e ^ {- i q y} \left[ 1 - e ^ {- i \Xi (y, t ^ {\prime})} \right] \right| ^ {2} d \theta , \tag {12}
$$

Making the substitutions of variables $|q|y \to y$ , $|q|z \to z$ and $\omega t' \to \tau$ , we get

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \frac {\hbar}{2 \pi P} \left| \int_ {- \infty} ^ {+ \infty} d y e ^ {- i s y} \left[ 1 - e ^ {- i \Xi (y, \tau)} \right] \right| ^ {2} \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}},
$$

$$
\Xi (y, \tau) = \frac {\zeta y Q}{2} \int_ {- \infty} ^ {\infty} \frac {d z}{r} \left[ J _ {1} (\zeta r) \sin (\phi) - N _ {1} (\zeta r) \cos (\phi) \right],
$$

$$
\zeta = \frac {k}{| q |} = \frac {\hbar k}{P \theta}, Q = \frac {e \Phi_ {0}}{2 c \hbar}, \phi = \tau + \frac {c}{v} \zeta z,
$$

$$
r = \sqrt {y ^ {2} + z ^ {2}}, \quad s = \operatorname{sgn} q. \tag {13

\tag {13}
$$

Recall that $\tau = \omega(t - x/v)$ and the cross section in the two-dimensional case has the dimension of length. Let us show that in the stationary case the expression (13) goes over to the known result. To do this, we set $\omega \to 0$ before the change of variables, and obtain the differential scattering cross section for the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect [1, 11]

$$
d \sigma = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P} \sin^ {2} Q \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}}. \tag {14}
$$

For $v/c \ll 1$ and $\zeta \lesssim 1$ the main contribution to the cross section is determined by the integration region $y \ll 1$ and $z \ll 1$ . In this case we find the asymptotics $\Xi_{0}(y, \tau)$ of the function $\Xi(y, \tau)$ ,

$$
\Xi_ {0} (y, \tau) = Q \operatorname{sgn} (y) \cos \tau \exp (- | y | / \vartheta),
$$

$$
\vartheta = \frac {v}{c \zeta} = \frac {\theta}{\theta_ {0}}, \quad \theta_ {0} = \frac {\hbar \omega}{2 E}. \tag {15}
$$

Using integration by parts and the asymptotics (15), we write the expression for the cross section in the form

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| s G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta} G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
\sigma_ {0} = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P \theta_ {0}} = \frac {2 v}{\pi \omega}, \quad \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) = Q e ^ {- y} \cos \tau . \tag {16}
$$

The function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ changes sign when replacing $\cos \tau \to -\cos \tau$ , while $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ does not change sign. For convenience, in Eq. (16) we have passed from the angle $\theta$ to $\vartheta$ .

If $\vartheta \gg 1$ ( $1 \gg \theta \gg \theta_0$ ), then

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \sin^ {2} (Q \cos \tau) \frac {d \vartheta}{\vartheta^ {2}}. \tag {17}

  "text": "\\tag {13}\n$$\n\nRecall that $\\tau = \\omega(t - x/v)$ and the cross section in the two-dimensional case has the dimension of length. Let us show that in the stationary case the expression (13) goes over to the known result. To do this, we set $\\omega \\to 0$ before the change of variables, and obtain the differential scattering cross section for the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect [1, 11]\n\n$$\nd \\sigma = \\frac {2 \\hbar}{\\pi P} \\sin^ {2} Q \\frac {d \\theta}{\\theta^ {2}}. \\tag {14}\n$$\n\nFor $v/c \\ll 1$ and $\\zeta \\lesssim 1$ the main contribution to the cross section is determined by the integration region $y \\ll 1$ and $z \\ll 1$ . In this case we find the asymptotics $\\Xi_{0}(y, \\tau)$ of the function $\\Xi(y, \\tau)$ ,\n\n$$\n\\Xi_ {0} (y, \\tau) = Q \\operatorname{sgn} (y) \\cos \\tau \\exp (- | y | / \\vartheta),\n$$\n\n$$\n\\vartheta = \\frac {v}{c \\zeta} = \\frac {\\theta}{\\theta_ {0}}, \\quad \\theta_ {0} = \\frac {\\hbar \\omega}{2 E}. \\tag {15}\n$$\n\nUsing integration by parts and the asymptotics (15), we write the expression for the cross section in the form\n\n$$\nd \\sigma (\\theta , \\tau) = \\sigma_ {0} \\left| s G _ {1} (\\vartheta , \\tau) + \\frac {1}{\\vartheta} G _ {2} (\\vartheta , \\tau) \\right| ^ {2} d \\vartheta ,\n$$\n\n$$\nG _ {1} (\\vartheta , \\tau) = \\int_ {0} ^ {\\infty} d y \\sin [ \\Xi_ {1} (y, \\tau) ] \\sin (\\vartheta y),\n$$\n\n$$\nG _ {2} (\\vartheta , \\tau) = \\int_ {0} ^ {\\infty} d y \\Xi_ {1} (y, \\tau) \\sin [ \\Xi_ {1} (y, \\tau) ] \\sin (\\vartheta y),\n$$\n\n$$\n\\sigma_ {0} = \\frac {2 \\hbar}{\\pi P \\theta_ {0}} = \\frac {2 v}{\\pi \\omega}, \\quad \\Xi_ {1} (y, \\tau) = Q e ^ {- y} \\cos \\tau . \\tag {16}\n$$\n\nThe function $G_{1}(\\vartheta, \\tau)$ changes sign when replacing $\\cos \\tau \\to -\\cos \\tau$ , while $G_{2}(\\vartheta, \\tau)$ does not change sign. For convenience, in Eq. (16) we have passed from the angle $\\theta$ to $\\vartheta$ .\n\nIf $\\vartheta \\gg 1$ ( $1 \\gg \\theta \\gg \\theta_0$ ), then\n\n$$\nd \\sigma (\\vartheta , \\tau) = \\sigma_ {0} \\sin^ {2} (Q \\cos \\tau) \\frac {d \\vartheta}{\\vartheta^ {2}}. \\tag {17}",

This equation is independent of spin orientation. Using the method of characteristics, we find the solution to Eq. (9),

$$
F (x, y, t) = \exp \left\{- i \frac {e y}{c \hbar} \int_ {- \infty} ^ {x} d z \frac {A (x , y , t + (z - x) / v)}{\sqrt {y ^ {2} + z ^ {2}}} \right\}, \tag {10}
$$

where v = P/M. The shift in the last argument of the function $A(x, y, t)$ corresponds to the retardation effect. Note that the wave function (8), with $F(x,y,t)$ given by (10), satisfies the condition (6).

To obtain the scattering cross section, it is necessary to find the limit

$$
S (y, t ^ {\prime}) = \lim _ {x \to \infty} F (x, y, t)
$$

at a fixed value $t' = t - x / v$ . Replacing in (10) the upper limit of the integral with $\infty$ , we get

$$
S (y, t ^ {\prime}) = e ^ {- i \Xi (y, t ^ {\prime})},
$$

$$
\Xi (y, t ^ {\prime}) = \frac {e y}{c \hbar} \int_ {- \infty} ^ {\infty} \frac {d z}{\sqrt {y ^ {2} + z ^ {2}}}   A (x, y, t ^ {\prime} + z / v)  , \tag {11}
$$

Since the field is time-dependent, the particle flux (cross-section $d\sigma$ ) is also time-dependent. Following the standard derivation, see [11], we obtain

$$
d \sigma (\theta , t) = \frac {P}{2 \pi \hbar} \left| \int_ {- \infty} ^ {+ \infty} d y e ^ {- i q y} \left[ 1 - e ^ {- i \Xi (y, t ^ {\prime})} \right] \right| ^ {2} d \theta , \tag {12}
$$

Making the substitutions of variables $|q|y \to y$ , $|q|z \to z$ and $\omega t' \to \tau$ , we get

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \frac {\hbar}{2 \pi P} \left| \int_ {- \infty} ^ {+ \infty} d y e ^ {- i s y} \left[ 1 - e ^ {- i \Xi (y, \tau)} \right] \right| ^ {2} \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}},
$$

$$
\Xi (y, \tau) = \frac {\zeta y Q}{2} \int_ {- \infty} ^ {\infty} \frac {d z}{r} \left[ J _ {1} (\zeta r) \sin (\phi) - N _ {1} (\zeta r) \cos (\phi) \right],
$$

$$
\zeta = \frac {k}{| q |} = \frac {\hbar k}{P \theta}, Q = \frac {e \Phi_ {0}}{2 c \hbar}, \phi = \tau + \frac {c}{v} \zeta z,
$$

$$
r = \sqrt {y ^ {2} + z ^ {2}}, \quad s = \operatorname{sgn} q. \tag {13

Replacing in (10) the upper limit of the integral with $\\infty$ , we get\n\n$$\nS (y, t ^ {\\prime}) = e ^ {- i \\Xi (y, t ^ {\\prime})},\n$$\n\n$$\n\\Xi (y, t ^ {\\prime}) = \\frac {e y}{c \\hbar} \\int_ {- \\infty} ^ {\\infty} \\frac {d z}{\\sqrt {y ^ {2} + z ^ {2}}}   A (x, y, t ^ {\\prime} + z / v)  , \\tag {11}\n$$\n\nSince the field is time-dependent, the particle flux (cross-section $d\\sigma$ ) is also time-dependent.
